 \section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

-Recent evidence has linked occupational exposure to generative artificial intelligence with weaker employment growth among workers in their early twenties. The pattern matters because it bears on whether new technology is already changing entry-level work. It is also unusually hard to interpret. Occupational exposure is fixed before the labor outcome is observed, is highly correlated with broad occupational structure, and is available in several constructed forms that do not necessarily measure the same object. A negative exposed-versus-unexposed contrast can therefore reflect detailed AI susceptibility, broad occupational composition, or both.
+Recent evidence associates generative-AI exposure with weaker employment growth among workers in their early twenties.  Interpretation is difficult because exposure is constructed rather than observed, high- and low-exposure jobs differ broadly, and the period around ChatGPT's release includes several aggregate disruptions.

-This paper asks a deliberately narrower question: \emph{does a detailed occupational AI score explain the public-CPS early-career employment pattern beyond the evolution of broad occupation families?} I aggregate Basic Monthly Current Population Survey records into occupation-by-age-by-month employment stocks from January 2017 through July 2026. The design compares ages 22--25 with ages 26--65 within occupations and asks whether that relative stock changes after January 2023 across exposure groups. The outcome is a stock, not hiring, and the design is descriptive. It does not observe employer adoption and does not turn the release of ChatGPT into an instrument.
+This paper asks which occupational comparisons support the public Current Population Survey (CPS) pattern.  I rebuild monthly employment stocks from January 2017 through July 2026 and classify occupations with the GPT-4 beta score of \citet{eloundou2024gpts}.  The target is the post-December-2022 change in the log conditional-mean employment-stock ratio of ages 22--25 to ages 26--65 in the highest relative to the lowest exposure quintile, conditional on intermediate quintiles and preperiod Webb software exposure.  I examine broad-family paths, direct within-family support, preperiod computer use, dynamics, and linked-CPS flows.  Exposure measures susceptibility rather than adoption; January 2023 is chronology, not an instrument.

-The corrected-calendar baseline reproduces a sizeable pattern. The post-January-2023 young-relative coefficient for the highest versus lowest employment-weighted quintile of Eloundou et al.'s GPT-4 beta exposure is $-0.1346$ log points, with a canonical 9,999-draw 95-percent wild-score interval of $[-0.2223,-0.0468]$. The frozen 108-month chronology gives $-0.1311$ and its canonical interval $[-0.2171,-0.0451]$. These numbers describe the change in a young-to-older employment-stock ratio, not a percentage decline in individual employment probability.
+Direct support is thin.  Four families span both exposure tails; their 29 occupations represent 5.03 percent of preperiod stock, and the interval includes large effects of either sign.  The conditioned comparison otherwise relies on intermediate quintiles and common-coefficient restrictions, so its near-zero estimate is not economic equivalence or evidence of no AI effect.

-The central result changes the interpretation. Adding two-digit-occupation-family by young by post interactions reduces the corrected coefficient to $-0.0315$ with interval $[-0.1676,0.1046]$. Allowing every broad family its own young-relative monthly path gives $-0.0317$. The paired baseline-to-conditioned movement is about $0.103$ log points and its interval excludes zero. Yet this is not an additive decomposition: the conditioning model asks whether detailed quintile status predicts outcomes among the comparisons left after broad-family evolution is absorbed. Only four of 22 broad families contain both Q1 and Q5, and conditional target information falls to 30 percent of the baseline. The evidence says that the baseline is predominantly a comparison across broad occupation families and that the remaining within-family association is weakly measured. It does not assign a causal share to broad composition.
+The reconstructed coefficient is $-0.132$ log points, with 95-percent occupation wild-score interval $[-0.221,-0.044]$; it is neither an individual probability nor an employer hiring rate.  With two-digit occupation-family young-relative monthly paths, it is $-0.022$ ($[-0.161,0.117]$).  Their paired difference is $0.110$ ($[0.011,0.210]$).  This distinguishes conditional estimands without making a causal decomposition: family paths may absorb AI-related and non-AI-related evolution.  The coefficient movement is therefore a consequence of the support result, not evidence that one known share of an AI effect is explained by composition.

-Nor does a simple food-service story account for the result. Excluding low-exposure food-service occupations attenuates the coefficient by 8.8 percent, to $-0.1196$. Excluding food, personal-care, and protective-service occupations makes it slightly more negative. Jointly removing the five occupations with the largest prior leave-one-out movements yields $-0.1011$, while symmetric trimming and continuous down-weighting return estimates close to baseline. Influence is consequential and signed in both directions; ``food-service recovery explains the pattern'' is not supported.
+Annual ACS data separate person-sampling resolution from occupational support.
+With fixed primary exposure labels, the full-calendar family-year coefficient is negative
+with an ACS replicate interval excluding zero, but occupation- and family-shock
+intervals include zero; the nonreuse-calendar family-year coefficient is also
+undetected.  More respondents therefore narrow one uncertainty component
+without creating the missing within-family tail comparisons.

-The second result concerns precision. The realized occupation-clustered standard error is 0.0444, compared with 0.0122 in the planning simulation. The frozen design's normal-theory minimum detectable effect at 80-percent power is 0.1244 log points, approximately the size of the headline coefficient. Paired minimum detectable differences across alternative exposure constructions range from 0.0609 to 0.1689. Every paired Q5--Q1 interval comparing beta with an alternative contains zero. The data therefore do not detect architecture differences at conventional levels; they also do not establish that the architectures are economically equivalent. This distinction replaces earlier language that treated smaller or insignificant alternative point estimates as evidence that the conclusion did not generalize.
+Adding a pre-2022 O*NET computer-use measure makes the common-support coefficient more negative; one control therefore does not resolve computerization.  Unrestricted quarterly coefficients reject preperiod equality, while linked-flow intervals include zero.  The data neither isolate an AI counterfactual nor attribute the stock pattern to exit, switching, or entry allocation.

-The third result connects the public-data exercise directly to \citet{brynjolfsson2026canaries}. Implementing their published GPT-4 beta classification---the top two exposure quintiles against the bottom three---inside the same CPS model yields $-0.0728$ (interval $[-0.1240,-0.0216]$). This is a reproducible component of their design, not a replication of proprietary ADP outcomes, job-title mapping, firm controls, hiring, or separation. Reapplying the grouping across exposure architectures produces coefficients between approximately $-0.088$ and $-0.008$ on common support; only beta's difference from the OECD capability measure is detected under that grouping. The contrast itself is part of the empirical object.
-
-Measurement still matters, but in more specific ways than the earlier manuscript claimed. A literal SOC-2010 exact-code merge into post-2018 data covers only 3.33 percent of computer-and-mathematical employment; the documented taxonomy repair covers 97.7 percent. Most of the associated coefficient change comes from readmitting occupations rather than changing exposure values on fixed support. The original six implementations also span little independent variation: two weighted principal components explain 96.11 percent of their common-support variance. These facts motivate a taxonomy that separates implementation errors, same-construct measurement changes, construct changes, support changes, estimand changes, representation changes, and pure reparameterizations.
-
-The paper contributes a public-data diagnosis of a prominent descriptive pattern and a disciplined way to audit constructed treatments. Its substantive conclusion is negative but informative: in the CPS, detailed AI quintiles do not carry a precisely estimated association after broad occupational evolution is absorbed. Its measurement conclusion is equally bounded: alternative constructed scores can differ in coverage and classification while available outcomes remain too noisy to distinguish many downstream coefficients. The paper does not estimate a causal AI effect, infer economic equivalence from a confidence interval containing zero, or claim that broad-family conditioning identifies the non-AI counterfactual.
-
-The remainder of the paper defines the construction layers, documents the corrected data and inferential target, presents the broad-family result, and then evaluates architecture precision, reference groups, influence, population controls, and timing. Mobility and algebraic reparameterizations are demoted to the online appendix because they do not resolve the employment-stock question.
-
-\begin{figure}[!htbp]
-\centering
-\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figure1_architecture_genealogy_revised.pdf}
-\caption{From exposure construction to an economic statement}\label{fig:genealogy}
-\tabnote{The six initial implementations belong to two selected families. Webb AI and the OECD capability gap change the construct as well as the score. Mapping, support, representation, comparison, and conditioning determine the downstream estimand. Potential exposure is not observed adoption.}
-\end{figure}
+The paper shows that a sizeable public-CPS association is identified predominantly by comparisons spanning broad occupation families, quantifies the resolution of direct and flow evidence, and records calendar and taxonomy failures that silently alter support.  This localization tells future adoption or quasi-experimental studies which comparisons they must replace rather than inherit.  It narrows rather than overturns the motivating evidence: it neither replicates proprietary payroll outcomes nor identifies AI, computerization, or pandemic recovery as the cause.

-\section{Constructed Treatments and Related Evidence}\label{sec:literature}
+\section{Related Evidence and the Empirical Object}\label{sec:literature}

-\subsection{A layered empirical object}
+The closest motivating study is the August 2026 revision of \citet{brynjolfsson2026canaries}.  Using ADP payroll records through June 2026, it reports weaker employment growth among ages 22--25 in occupations with high GPT-4 beta exposure and studies employer-match hiring and separation.  The CPS cannot reproduce its employer panel, standardized-title mapping, firm controls, or worker--firm flows.  Because that study also reports public CPS and ACS evidence, novelty here lies in auditing a fixed national-CPS estimand, not discovering the age pattern.

-Write a constructed-treatment analysis as a sequence from an economic construct $C$, through a measurement rule $M$, a harmonization rule $H$, a retained support $\Omega$, and a representation $R$, to an estimand $Q$:
-\[
-C\xrightarrow{M}x^{N}\xrightarrow{H}x\xrightarrow{\Omega,w}x|_{\Omega}
-\xrightarrow{R}Z\xrightarrow{Q,P}\theta.
-\]
-Calling every perturbation a different ``measure'' conceals economically important distinctions. An invalid taxonomy merge is an implementation error. Reweighting the same application--ability links is a same-construct measurement change. Moving from LLM task acceleration to patent overlap or a broad capability gap changes the construct. Expanding mapped occupations changes the target population. Converting a level to quintiles changes the treatment representation. Replacing Q5--Q1 by Q5--Q2 changes the comparison. Rotating a fixed two-regressor column space changes only coordinates.
-
-Table~\ref{tab:architectures} gives the reporting taxonomy used throughout. A row can carry more than one label: repairing a mapping both corrects an implementation and may readmit occupations. The analysis separates the fixed-support score change from the support expansion rather than calling their sum a ``crosswalk effect.''
-
-\input{tables/table1_architectures.tex}
-
-Two simple invariance results discipline the exercise. First, for fixed positive weights and a fixed tie rule, a positive affine transformation preserves ranks and weighted-quantile membership. Second, if two regressor matrices are related by a nonsingular coordinate transformation and sample, objective, nuisance terms, and constraints remain fixed, fitted values and transformed contrasts are identical. The $F/G$ to $A/E$ exercise in the prior draft is therefore a basis change, not additional empirical corroboration; it is retained only in the online appendix.
-
-The task-family construction has an equally useful structure. If $D_o$ is the share of tasks directly accelerated by an LLM and $S_o$ the share accelerated only with complementary software, then
-\begin{equation}
-X_{\alpha o}=D_o,\qquad X_{\beta o}=D_o+\tfrac12S_o,\qquad X_{\gamma o}=D_o+S_o.\label{eq:ds}
-\end{equation}
-For any pair of occupations, rankings along $D+\lambda S$ can change only where their affine score paths cross. Weighted-bin assignments are consequently piecewise constant in $\lambda$ on fixed support. There is no reason, however, for estimated categorical coefficients to be monotone in $\lambda$.
-
-\subsection{Exposure architectures}
+Administrative and workplace studies observe adoption or tool access in particular settings \citep{humlum2025still,brynjolfsson2025generative}.  Firm-level evidence also distinguishes direct task substitution, reallocation across tasks, and firm-growth effects \citep{hampole2025artificial}.  Survey evidence shows that realized generative-AI use varies substantially even among workers doing similar work and that exposure explains only part of adoption \citep{bick2026work}.  Occupation scores instead describe tasks a technology might affect.  Remote-work evidence supplies another competing interpretation \citep{emanuel2026proximity}.  None turns a capability score into observed deployment.

-The core audit contains three AIOE implementations and three Eloundou implementations. AIOE links progress in ten AI applications to 52 O*NET abilities and then to occupational ability requirements \citep{felten2018method,felten2021occupational}. Administrative, direct-ability, and source-employment-weighted versions vary reconstruction or aggregation within that system. The Eloundou family changes the technology boundary through equation~\eqref{eq:ds} \citep{eloundou2024gpts}. These are six implementations from two families, not six independent measurements of a known latent exposure.
+The primary score is Eloundou et al.'s GPT-4 beta: tasks are labeled by whether an LLM, alone or with complementary software, could reduce completion time at constant quality \citep{eloundou2024gpts}.  AIOE maps AI applications to abilities \citep{felten2018method,felten2021occupational}; Webb measures patent--task overlap \citep{webb2020impact}; Dingel--Neiman classifies teleworkability \citep{dingel2020home}.  Computer use, routine content, education, and remotability are therefore overlapping occupational characteristics, not clean alternative treatments.  The task literature likewise allows technology to substitute for some tasks, complement others, and reallocate work within occupations \citep{autor2003skill,autor2013growth,acemoglu2019automation}.  Faster-changing technical careers may also combine early entry advantages with skill obsolescence later in the career \citep{deming2020earnings}, while the employment consequence of automating a task depends on whether the remaining bundle requires more or less expertise \citep{autor2025expertise}.  These mechanisms make an ages-22--25 versus older-worker contrast economically relevant but not uniquely diagnostic of entry-level AI substitution.

-The treatment-side dependence is strong. On the exact 444-occupation common support, the first weighted principal component explains 80.04 percent of standardized-score variance and the first two explain 96.11 percent; three explain 99.58 percent. The intersection--union sign calculation for the six coefficients is therefore retained only as a predeclared statement about a dependent set. It is not presented as six independent confirmations, and the failed simultaneous-band criterion remains in the design record.
+This distinction changes the burden of interpretation.  Agreement across correlated scores can show that a direction is not unique to one coding rule, but cannot validate a latent exposure construct.  Conversely, coefficient differences can arise from task definitions, occupational support, or treatment-group membership.  The empirical work below therefore matches supports where possible and names population changes where it is not.

-Two externally admitted constructions use different primitives. Webb maps patent text to occupational tasks, while the OECD measure compares nine capability domains with occupational requirements \citep{webb2020impact,oecd2026exposure}. They are different economic constructs, not reliability draws for the same LLM variable. Frey--Osborne remains an automation comparator rather than being relabeled as AI. A repository-bounded census records six core implementations from two families, two admitted external implementations from two additional families, one scope failure, and several published candidates whose score artifacts or mappings are not yet locally verified. This is not claimed to be a timeless census of the literature.
-
-\subsection{Relation to labor-market evidence}
-
-\citet{brynjolfsson2026canaries} use proprietary ADP payroll records and emphasize employment declines among ages 22--25 in highly exposed jobs, operating largely through hiring. Their public rule places GPT-4 beta occupations in employment-weighted quintiles and contrasts the top two with the bottom three. Section~\ref{sec:results} implements that public classification in CPS. It cannot recover their employer panel, title-to-occupation mapping, firm-time controls, hiring, or separations.
-
-Other studies answer different questions. \citet{humlum2025still} link reported adoption to Danish administrative outcomes; workplace deployments observe actual tool access rather than occupational potential \citep{brynjolfsson2025generative}. These designs distinguish exposure from use but do not validate one universal occupation score. The broader task literature likewise emphasizes substitution, complementarity, and occupational adjustment rather than a one-dimensional technology dose \citep{autor2003skill,autor2013growth,acemoglu2019automation}.
-
-This paper is closest in spirit to sensitivity analysis that asks which moments or trends support an empirical result \citep{andrews2017sensitivity,rambachan2023credible} and to work on generated covariates \citep{battaglia2024inference,christensen2026valid,duan2026inference,ludwig2026large}. Conventional occupation-clustered inference here conditions on labels, mapping, and support. The online appendix specifies what a validation sample would need to contain before generated-covariate corrections could address occupational AI exposure itself.
+I distinguish six layers:
+\[
+C\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow \Omega\longrightarrow R\longrightarrow Q,
+\]
+the intended construct, measurement rule, occupational harmonization, retained support, representation, and regression estimand.  A vintage mismatch is an implementation error; score repair on fixed support changes measurement; occupation readmission changes population; and a different exposure contrast changes the estimand.  Common-draw paired inference tests coefficient movement, while each component interval measures its own resolution.  Neither a detected movement nor a nondetection assigns a causal share or establishes equivalence.  This focus connects the exercise to specification-sensitivity and generated-covariate inference \citep{andrews2017sensitivity,rambachan2023credible,battaglia2024inference,christensen2026valid,duan2026inference,ludwig2026large}.

-\subsection{Taxonomy bridge and universe reconciliation}
+\input{tables/r3_table1_data.tex}

-CPS occupations use Census-2010 codes through 2019 and Census-2018 codes thereafter, while source exposures use SOC vintages. Pre-2020 records are expanded along official Census-2010-to-2018 routes and aggregated to the common target taxonomy. In a one-to-many route, official conversion proportions are applied equally to young and older source records. This preserves the source occupation's age ratio across target components by construction; official files do not supply age-specific probabilities.
+\subsection{Harmonization and support}

-The full route-expanded preperiod universe contains 539 target occupations with positive young and older stock. Two counts that appeared to be a five-occupation discrepancy are instead nonnested, architecture-specific supports. The stored 490-occupation universe requires full beta coverage. The 495-occupation continuous-mapping universe requires repaired AIOE and Webb coverage. They overlap in 466 occupations: 24 appear only in beta support and 29 only in AIOE-plus-Webb support. Adding Webb to beta leaves the 468-occupation primary model. The online appendix names every member and reason.
+Pre-2020 Census-2010 occupations are routed to Census-2018 using versioned official shares.  Applying the same shares to young and older records preserves source stock and age ratios by construction, but does not validate age-specific routing.  The route reaches 99.546 percent of eligible early stock and conserves routed weight to machine precision.

-Within primary support, split sources contribute 14.93 percent of young and 17.75 percent of older weighted stock during 2017--2019. Ninety-nine target occupations can receive a split route and carry 23.38 percent of fitted target information; proportional allocation of information by split-stock share assigns 4.44 percent to split stock. The former is a conservative touched-target diagnostic and the latter is not an identified decomposition.
+Primary support requires finite GPT-4 beta and Webb software scores and positive preperiod young and older stock.  It contains 468 occupations and 87.19 percent of candidate-universe stock; a named file records all 73 exclusions.  Raw AIOE is a standardized source-occupation index, not a percentage; the source workbook has mean approximately zero, unit sample standard deviation, and an interquartile difference of 1.879 raw points.  A literal SOC-2010 AIOE merge into post-2018 employment data covers only 3.33 percent of computer-and-mathematical employment, versus 97.7 percent after version-aware routing.  Fixed-support score repair changes little; occupation readmission changes the population.  I treat the literal merge as invalid and do not attribute it to another paper without implementation evidence.

-The negative association does not depend on split routes. Restricting the corrected model to 369 targets with no structurally possible split inbound route gives $-0.1674$ under fixed labels and $-0.1471$ after recomputing the comparison on that support. Aggregating to a stable Census-2010 taxonomy gives $-0.1522$. Each changes the population and should not be called a correction of the primary estimate.
+The support file separates missing exposure, missing Webb, and absent young preperiod stock.  This matters because a common-support regression estimates a population defined by all required scores, whereas native-support rows can change both the exposure definition and who remains.  The appendix's four-step crosswalk table holds support fixed before adding newly reachable occupations, so measurement correction is not conflated with sample composition.

-Age-specific route allocation remains uncertain. Under unrestricted official-route accounting, the 2017--2019 young/older stock ratio can range from .1031 to .1480 in Q1 and from .0790 to .1175 in Q5. These are sharp accounting ranges for stocks, not bounds on the nonlinear regression coefficient. A predeclared tilt that changes young-versus-older allocation odds toward the highest-beta eligible target by factors from .5 to 2 moves the coefficient from $-0.1292$ to $-0.1391$. Only 6.88 percent of early stock is eligible for that scenario, so it is not a global robustness claim.

 \begin{equation}
-E[N_{oat}|X]=\exp\!\left[\gamma_{oa}+\delta_{ot}+\lambda_{at}
-+\sum_{q=2}^{5}\beta_q Q_{oq}Y_aP_t+\theta W_oY_aP_t\right].\label{eq:headline}
+E[N_{oat}\mid X]=\exp\!\left[\gamma_{oa}+\delta_{ot}+\lambda_{at}
++\sum_{q=2}^{5}\beta_q Q_{oq}Y_aP_t+\theta W_oY_aP_t\right].
+\label{eq:headline}
 \end{equation}
-Occupation-by-age, occupation-by-month, and age-by-month fixed effects absorb time-invariant age composition within occupation, common occupation shocks, and aggregate age-specific shocks. The target $\beta_5$ is the change in the young-to-older employment-stock ratio for Q5 relative to Q1 after January 2023, conditional on Q2--Q4 and Webb software exposure. It is not an individual employment probability, a hiring rate, or a continuous exposure slope.
+The target $\beta_5$ is the postperiod change in the young-to-older stock ratio for Q5 relative to Q1, conditional on Q2--Q4 and Webb.  It is not an individual probability, continuous slope, or hiring rate.

-With two age groups, conditioning independent Poisson pseudo-likelihood contributions on $T_{ot}=N_{oyt}+N_{oot}$ gives
+With two age groups, conditioning on $T_{ot}=N_{oyt}+N_{oot}$ removes the occupation--month effect and gives
 \begin{equation}
 \ell_{ot}=N_{oyt}\log p_{ot}+N_{oot}\log(1-p_{ot}),\qquad
-\operatorname{logit}(p_{ot})=\log(\mu_{oyt}/\mu_{oot}).\label{eq:conditional}
+\operatorname{logit}(p_{ot})=\log(\mu_{oyt}/\mu_{oot}).

 \end{equation}
-The occupation-month effect cancels. CPS final weights construct the two continuous cell stocks and are not applied a second time to the objective. The grouped-binomial expression is an estimating equation for weighted stocks, not a literal model of independent survey-weighted trials.
-
-The central conditioning exercise augments equation~\eqref{eq:headline} with SOC2-family-specific $Y_aP_t$ terms. A saturated version instead absorbs SOC2-family-specific young-relative effects in every month. These models ask whether detailed exposure groups predict outcomes after broad-family evolution is conditioned out. They do not decompose the baseline coefficient into causal AI and non-AI components, because the conditioning set and therefore the estimand change.
+Final weights construct continuous cell stocks once; equation~\eqref{eq:conditional} is an estimating criterion, not a literal independent-binomial sampling model.

-\subsection{Maintained source of randomness}
+The central alternative gives each SOC two-digit family its own young-relative calendar-month path; a less saturated companion uses family-specific post shifts.  Both change the conditional estimand.  Neither the residual coefficient nor its movement from equation~\eqref{eq:headline} is an AI remainder or composition share.

-The principal confidence interval treats occupation-level economic score contributions as independent across 468 occupation clusters and permits arbitrary serial dependence within occupation. It conditions on the observed CPS-weighted cells, constructed exposure labels, taxonomy mapping, support, quintile cuts, and fitted specification. Thus it is model-based uncertainty for a stochastic occupational-shock parameter. It is not a complete design-based CPS variance estimator and does not cover exposure-label generation, taxonomy-allocation uncertainty, or every dependence induced by respondents moving across occupations.
+The occupational-conditioning, influence, dynamic, and flow analyses added in this revision were designed after the initial outcome pattern was known.  They are post-outcome exploratory analyses, not preregistered confirmatory tests; simultaneous inference does not undo that selection.

-For transparency, let $x_{ot}$ be the target and nuisance regressors after the grouped-binomial fit, $p_{ot}$ the fitted young share, and $h_{ot}=T_{ot}p_{ot}(1-p_{ot})$. The implementation absorbs the fixed effects from $x_{ot}$ under weights $h_{ot}$, producing $\widetilde x_{ot}$. It forms the information matrix
-\[
-H=\sum_{o,t}h_{ot}\widetilde x_{ot}\widetilde x_{ot}',
-\]
-the occupation score $s_o=\sum_t\widetilde x_{ot}(N_{oyt}-T_{ot}p_{ot})$, and the one-step influence $H^{-1}s_o$, with the finite-cluster factor $\sqrt{G/(G-1)}$. Each wild-score draw multiplies the entire occupation influence vector by one Rademacher sign. The analytic occupation-cluster standard error studentizes the centered draw; the 95-percent critical value is the empirical .95 quantile of the absolute studentized shifts. Paired comparisons reuse the same signs for both fits, thereby preserving estimated covariance. The procedure does not create pseudo-outcomes or refit each draw.
+\subsection{What the design establishes}

-All central revised intervals use 9,999 multipliers. The sole canonical interval for the frozen $-0.1311$ estimate is $[-0.2171,-0.0451]$; previously displayed variants came from different finite sets of 999 multipliers, except a minimum-count row that changed the sample. Pair-specific member intervals remain labeled as such and never replace the canonical interval.
+The design measures the magnitude, support, and specification sensitivity of a public-CPS association.  It distinguishes paired coefficients and locates the occupational comparisons carrying the contrast, but cannot allocate movement among AI, computerization, pandemic recovery, or other forces.  Attenuation is not a causal share, survival is not an AI remainder, and an interval containing zero is nondetection rather than economic equivalence.  The limitations section returns to preperiod evolution, sparse direct support, exposure without adoption, and incomplete flow and survey-design information.

-\subsection{Precision, sparse cells, and rotation sensitivity}
+For shared observations I reuse bootstrap multipliers and form each draw of $\Delta=\widehat\beta^{(2)}-\widehat\beta^{(1)}$.  A paired interval excluding zero shows statistical distinguishability under that procedure; it does not identify the reason.  An interval including zero means nondetection, not economic equivalence.  I report $\mathrm{MDE}_{80}\simeq(1.96+0.84)SE$ only as a precision diagnostic.

-The realized primary SE is 0.0444, versus 0.0122 in the planning simulation. Its normal-theory 80-percent minimum detectable effect is therefore 0.1244 log points, or 13.25 percent on the relative-stock-ratio scale. The paper uses MDEs to characterize what the design can resolve; it does not substitute an arbitrary smallest effect of interest or interpret a confidence interval containing zero as equivalence.
-
-Young cells are sparse: the 10th, 50th, and 90th percentiles of respondent-equivalent counts are 0, 2, and 16; 26.3 percent are zero and 69.9 percent are below five. Older-cell percentiles are 4, 25, and 199. One-sided cells contribute valid finite terms to equation~\eqref{eq:conditional}; only 965 occupation-months with both stocks zero are omitted. Conditioning on at least five observed young respondents attenuates the coefficient to about $-0.109$, but this selects on realized outcomes, retains fewer than half of occupation-months, and changes the estimand.
+To describe where a target is learned, let $h_{ot}=T_{ot}p_{ot}(1-p_{ot})$ and let $r_{ot}$ be the target regressor after projection on every nuisance regressor in the fitted-information metric.  I report
+\[
+I=\sum_{o,t}h_{ot}r_{ot}^{2},\qquad
+s_o=\frac{\sum_t h_{ot}r_{ot}^{2}}{I},\qquad
+G_{\mathrm{eff}}=\left(\sum_o s_o^2\right)^{-1}.
+\]
+These are fitted target information and concentration diagnostics, not an effective number of CPS sampling clusters.  Family and occupation shares, leave-one-out estimates, and the top-five information share are reported without using influence to select a preferred sample.

-Quarterly aggregation gives $-0.1310$ under the frozen chronology and $-0.1345$ under the repaired chronology, essentially matching monthly estimates and SEs. To probe CPS rotation and common monthly shocks, a post-outcome score-covariance sensitivity adds contemporaneous and lagged covariance in aggregate month scores while subtracting the occupation-month intersection. At a 12-month Newey--West lag the SE rises to 0.0491 and the normal interval is $[-0.2273,-0.0349]$. Occupation clustering already permits arbitrary within-occupation serial dependence; this extra calculation addresses declared cross-occupation time covariance but remains model-based rather than a CPS replicate-weight variance.
+The principal 9,999-draw wild-score interval treats occupations as shock clusters and allows arbitrary serial dependence within occupation.  It conditions on weighted cells, exposure labels, mapping, support, and specification.  Separate sensitivities use 22 broad-family shocks, household/sample-unit full refits under released weights, and elapsed-calendar time-HAC scores.  A sparse-cell and family-shock simulation audits finite-sample behavior.  These procedures have different stochastic targets; they are not mechanically combined, and none supplies complete CPS design inference or exposure-label uncertainty.

-Finally, a historical simulation that preserves each donor month's entire contemporaneous cross-occupation residual vector under a sharp global-sign design closes none of the prospective-to-realized precision gap. It rules out that specific implementation as the explanation; it does not validate the planning calculation or identify which unmodeled dependence generated the gap.
+The household exercise keeps every month and fractional route descendant of a positive CPS household identifier together.  It still omits unavailable multistage-selection and calibration uncertainty.  The time-HAC construction uses elapsed calendar lags, so the missing October 2025 is not treated as adjacency between September and November.

-This is strong evidence about the comparison and weak evidence about a structural mechanism. In point-estimate terms, conditioning absorbs about 76 percent of the baseline magnitude. Only four of 22 SOC2 families contain both Q1 and Q5, and conditional target information falls from 26.4 million to 8.0 million, about 30 percent of baseline. The conditional coefficient is connected through intermediate quintiles in most families. It should not be described as ``the remaining AI effect,'' and the difference should not be described as the causal contribution of broad composition.

-\subsection{Profile, reference tail, and stable classifications}
+The ratio $0.1104/0.1321$ is not a causal ``share explained.''  Broad families differ in AI-relevant tasks, computerization, education, work location, and pandemic recovery; family paths absorb all such common evolution.  The result says that the pooled estimate relies mainly on comparisons across families, not what would have happened without AI.

-The fitted post coefficients relative to Q1 are $0$, $-0.0855$, $-0.0478$, $-0.0970$, and $-0.1311$. A three-restriction test does not reject a common coefficient for Q2--Q5 ($p=.119$), but cannot establish their equality. The visible Q3--Q2 increase also does not yield a rejection of monotone decline under a least-favorable max-$t$ test ($p=.393$). The data therefore neither establish nor reject monotonicity.
+The full Q2--Q5 profile reaches the same conclusion.  Pooled coefficients are jointly different from zero but do not establish a monotone dose response.  After family-month conditioning, the four coefficients are jointly indistinguishable from zero.  Simultaneous intervals and paired movements appear in the online appendix.

-Changing the requested contrast clarifies Q1's role. Q5--Q2 is $-0.0456$ with interval $[-0.1248,0.0335]$; Q5--Q4 is $-0.0340$ with interval $[-0.1110,0.0429]$; Q5--Q3 is $-0.0833$ with interval $[-0.1581,-0.0084]$. These are different economic contrasts, not alternative omitted-category parameterizations. Figure~\ref{fig:paths} shows why: both the low-exposure trajectory and the older-worker denominator matter. The fitted contrast is not simply a collapse of young employment in Q5.

-\subsection{Influence and the food-service hypothesis}
+A continuous companion instead subtracts each family's preperiod-weighted beta mean and imposes a common within-family slope.  Its family-month coefficient is $-0.0025$ per 0.1084 raw beta units ($[-0.0240,0.0191]$).  It uses more residual variation but trades the tail contrast for linearity and a common slope.  Together, the specifications expose the support--functional-form tradeoff.

-Influence is economically consequential but not one-directional. Jointly deleting the five occupations with the largest absolute prior leave-one-out movements reduces the estimate to $-0.1011$. Deleting the top ten or twenty instead makes it more negative, $-0.1522$ and $-0.1555$, because later occupations have opposing signed influence. A symmetric 2.5-percent trim of each signed-influence tail gives $-0.1281$, and Huber-style continuous down-weighting gives $-0.1314$. These are outcome-adaptive diagnostics rather than preferred specifications.
+The pre-2020 occupational bridge adds a separate allocation uncertainty.  A
+jointly feasible odds-tilt grid reallocates the 14.86 percent of supported
+early-period stock routed through 31 split source occupations while preserving
+source--age--month mass.  With exposure groups rebuilt after each allocation,
+the pooled coefficient ranges from $-0.1446$ to $-0.1163$, and all six paired
+intervals relative to the no-tilt allocation contain zero.  The family-month
+estimand is less stable: at the $K=0.25$ endpoint it moves from $-0.0217$ to
+$0.0439$, a paired change of $0.0656$ ($[0.0031,0.1280]$), and five of six
+nonbaseline paired intervals exclude zero.  A wider 25-point feasible grid
+places the pooled coefficient between $-0.1599$ and $-0.0702$.  These are
+post-outcome mapping sensitivities over finite declared grids, not estimates of
+true age-specific routes, sharp bounds, or identified sets.

-Cleaner occupation-side exclusions do not support the strong claim that food-service recovery explains the headline. Dropping the five Q1 food-service occupations yields $-0.1196$, an 8.8-percent reduction in absolute magnitude. Dropping all food-service occupations gives $-0.1201$. Dropping all food, building-and-grounds, personal-care, and protective-service occupations yields $-0.1370$; dropping only their Q1 members gives $-0.1387$. These exercises cannot isolate reopening, immigration, minimum wages, or labor supply, but they reject the simpler assertion that removing in-person service occupations makes the pattern disappear.
+\subsection{Influence and observed paths}

+The online appendix reports nuisance-adjusted information, effective information counts, and family and occupation deletions.  Fast-food and counter workers have substantial signed influence because low-exposure service employment recovered differently after the pandemic, but other occupations push oppositely.  Outcome-selected deletion is not a design correction; symmetric trimming and economically defined service exclusions remain diagnostics.

+$-0.1321$, and neither component is an individual probability or a causal

-\subsection{Primitive models rather than rotated labels}
-
-The prior draft emphasized ``consensus'' and ``disagreement'' coordinates $F=(A+E)/2$ and $G=(A-E)/2$. Those coordinates are retained in the online appendix only as a complete record. A representative two-score model is more transparent: administrative AIOE enters at $+0.0449$ and beta at $-0.0788$ when included jointly. In direct task units, $D$ and $S$ enter at $-0.0323$ and $-0.0303$. These are conditional associations among correlated constructed regressors, not causal technology channels. Transforming $F/G$ back to $A/E$ reproduces the same fitted predictor exactly and supplies no additional evidence.
+With 22 family clusters, the paired movement remains detected under Rademacher and Webb weights; the Webb interval is $[0.0059,0.2150]$.  The completed finite-sample audit finds small coefficient bias but target-specific interval performance.  In the empirical observed-effect design, occupation-wild coverage is 0.865 for the pooled coefficient, 0.921 for the family-conditioned coefficient, and 0.889 for their paired movement; family clustering is conservative for the pooled and paired targets but covers the family-conditioned target only 0.895 of the time.  In the clean zero-target design, rejection rates for the family-conditioned coefficient are 6.1 percent under occupation weights, 8.9 percent under family weights, and 5.6 percent for the cross-fit oracle.  No one procedure is therefore validated for all targets and declared designs.  In the rebuilt-treatment audit, elapsed-calendar HAC leaves scalar uncertainty similar and none of the 15 five-parameter covariance matrices across three targets and five lags is indefinite.  Earlier indefinite matrices belong to the superseded historical-treatment audit and remain labeled in the archive.

-\section{Timing, Weights, and Remaining Interpretations}\label{sec:reallocation}
+\section{Dynamics, Flows, and External Comparison}\label{sec:dynamics}

-\subsection{Pre-AI pseudo-breaks and post-period dynamics}
+\subsection{Dynamics}

-The authenticated extract begins in January 2017, so the literal request for monthly pseudo-breaks beginning in 2015 is infeasible. I estimate every feasible break in the fully pre-AI January 2017--December 2019 window and emphasize 12 dates with at least 12 retained months on each side. Under both pre-AI classifications and the frozen classification, the balanced pseudo-break distributions are centered near zero; none approaches $-0.1311$. Because the 12 estimates overlap and were designed after the headline was known, the plus-one empirical tail of $1/13$ is a resolution floor, not a randomization $p$-value. Unbalanced early-window extremes are artifacts of having as little as one pre-break month.
+The binary post coefficient is not an event-study design.  I estimate quarterly coefficients for all four nonreference quintiles, omitting 2022Q4 (October--November; December is the transition month).  The Q5 path has 23 preperiod and 15 postperiod coefficients in pooled and family-month specifications.

-The actual post path remains descriptive rather than a causal event study. A model with separate periods gives $-0.1356$ in 2023, $-0.0783$ in 2024, and $-0.1629$ in 2025--26. The last is distinguishable from 2024 but not from 2023. No detected pretrend and an end-2022 break do not isolate AI from the technology-sector correction, interest rates, return-to-office changes, post-pandemic normalization, or occupational composition.
+Figure~\ref{fig:dynamics} shows the rebuilt Q5 path.  Relative to the omitted
+2022Q4 coefficient, joint tests reject that all 23 preperiod coefficients are
+zero in the pooled ($p=.0171$) and family-conditioned ($p=.0053$) models.  A
+reference-invariant test of equality within the entire preperiod rejects for
+the pooled path ($p=.0121$) but not for the family-conditioned path
+($p=.0641$).  The appendix reports fixed pre-pandemic, post-reopening, and
+pandemic-exclusion windows.  An insignificant anchored linear slope cannot
+override the unrestricted pooled test, while the family result illustrates
+the importance of the unusually short 2022Q4 reference.  Neither pattern
+supports a causal January-2023 interpretation.
+When 2020Q2--2020Q4 are excluded, the pooled test no longer rejects at five
+percent under either the short-reference null ($p=.0772$) or the
+reference-invariant within-window null ($p=.0676$).  For the family-conditioned
+path the corresponding values are $p=.0079$ and $p=.1048$, underscoring that
+the conclusion depends on the null as well as the calendar window.

-I do not add a national adoption series merely to replace the post indicator. Interacting one national series with fixed occupation exposure would largely reparameterize an already displayed exposure-by-time path. Industry-level BTOS adoption would require an occupation--industry ecological bridge and would still not generate exogenous occupational adoption. Such data could support a separately designed adoption paper, but forcing it into this measurement-centered revision would change both treatment and estimand without resolving the main confound.
+\begin{figure}[!htbp]
+\centering
+\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{r3_figure2_dynamics.pdf}
+\caption{Quarterly Q5--Q1 young-relative employment coefficients}\label{fig:dynamics}
+\tabnote{Coefficients are relative to Q1 and omitted 2022Q4 under rebuilt labels; lines are pointwise 95-percent occupation wild-score intervals.  The online appendix reports simultaneous bands and the Q2--Q5 system.  The conditioned model gives each SOC2 family its own young-relative monthly path.}
+\end{figure}

-\subsection{Population controls and response composition}
+Three related targets separate a genuine estimand comparison from an event-time
+normalization artifact.  The static grouped-binomial coefficient $S$ is
+$-0.1321$ pooled and $-0.0217$ after family conditioning.  The calendar-
+weighted post functional relative to omitted 2022Q4, $P$, is $-0.1199$
+($[-0.2637,0.0239]$) and $-0.2074$ ($[-0.4252,0.0103]$).  By contrast, the
+reference-invariant post-minus-pre functional $D$ is $-0.1314$ and $-0.0217$.
+The paired $D-S$ differences are 0.0007 ($p=.454$) and less than 0.0001 in
+absolute value ($p=.995$).  Thus $D$ nearly reproduces the static estimates;
+the design does not detect the family-model $P-S$ difference at five percent
+($-0.1858$, $p=.074$).  What it does detect is that family conditioning changes
+$P-S$ by $-0.1980$ ($p=.021$), while it does not detect a change in $D-S$
+($-0.0008$, $p=.693$).  The short-reference functional therefore responds to
+conditioning differently from the reference-invariant comparison.  This
+numerical reconciliation is not an equivalence claim and does not make any
+estimand causal.

-January 2025 introduced unusually large CPS population-control revisions. A literal age-by-occupation counterfactual series ``without'' those controls cannot be constructed: BLS provides aggregate experimental adjustments, not subgroup weights, and official earlier microdata are not revised. I therefore report what the available data identify rather than rescale cells ad hoc.
+Official \texttt{HonestDiD} 0.2.8 applies smoothness and relative-magnitude restrictions to the full event vector and covariance for $P$, not for $S$ or $D$.  Both the full 2017Q1--2022Q3 calibration and a consecutive 2021Q1--2022Q3 post-reopening calibration are reported.  Under the latter, the family-conditioned smoothness interval is $[-0.772,-0.007]$ when differential-trend bias is restricted to be exactly linear ($M=0$), but expands to $[-1.100,0.207]$ at the first positive grid value, $M=0.005$.  Both conventional $P$ intervals already include zero, so no positive zero-exclusion threshold exists.  The exercise cannot repair rejected preperiod equality or identify adoption.

-On the repaired calendar, official weights give $-0.1346$ and respondent-equivalent cells give $-0.1348$. Ending in December 2024 gives $-0.1135$ and $-0.1056$, respectively. In a joint official-weight model, 2023--24 is $-0.1108$ and 2025--26 is $-0.1664$; their paired difference is $-0.0556$ with interval $[-0.1228,0.0116]$. Under respondent equivalents the era difference is more negative and detected. The raw official-weight Q5--Q1 log-ratio contrast becomes slightly less negative from December 2024 to January 2025. These checks do not provide a counterfactual weight vintage, but they are inconsistent with revised population weights mechanically creating the full association.
+Onset estimates from November 2022 through June 2023 are similar within each structure, but common timing does not distinguish AI from coincident shocks.  Ending in December 2024 gives a pooled coefficient of $-0.111$ ($[-0.201,-0.021]$); its paired movement relative to the July-2026 estimate is $0.021$ ($[-0.007,0.049]$).  The corresponding movements at the September- and December-2025 endpoints are 0.0225 and 0.0212, essentially the same magnitude despite different interval endpoints.  The full window remains the canonical target and December 2024 a prominent pre-production-break benchmark; neither is causally clean.  Splitting the postperiod instead gives $-0.111$ in 2023--24 and $-0.168$ in 2025--26; their paired difference is $-0.057$ ($[-0.125,0.012]$), so the official-weight design does not detect a change between eras.  Family-conditioned endpoint changes remain near zero, and survey-gap exclusions are small.  A coding-stable post-2020 fit gives $-0.118$ pooled and $-0.030$ with family-month effects; their paired movements from the full-window models are $0.014$ ($[-0.029,0.057]$) and $-0.009$ ($[-0.105,0.088]$).  Quintile- and occupation-specific month-of-year controls move either coefficient by at most $0.0011$.  All six replacement fits pass the same numerical certification; the earlier failed attempts remain in the failure registry rather than being treated as results.

-``Respondent equivalent'' means that cells are reconstructed from observed employed records after route expansion without their CPS final weights. It changes the empirical cell outcome; dividing all weighted stocks by a common constant would not change the maximizer and is not what is done here.
+\subsection{Linked CPS margins}

-\subsection{Age, entry, and mobility}
+Table~\ref{tab:flows} estimates distinct adjacent- and twelve-month margins using validated links and official longitudinal weights.  Every primary interval includes zero.  Normal-theory MDE$_{80}$ values range from 0.115 to 0.336 log point, so these are low-resolution nondetections rather than evidence of absent flow responses.

-The pooled older comparison is not the sole source of the pattern. Ages 22--25 versus 26--35 give $-0.1307$, nearly identical to the frozen pooled result. Comparisons with ages 26--45 and 36--55 are $-0.1475$ and $-0.1464$. The contrast against ages 51--65 is smaller, $-0.0978$, and its paired difference from the pooled comparison is detected. Age 22--25 is an age group, not observed experience; the paper therefore uses ``young'' rather than treating every respondent as a labor-market entrant.
+\input{tables/r3_table5_flows.tex}

-The main outcome cannot determine whether hiring, separation, or occupation switching generated the stock association. CPS longitudinal links do allow distinct flow estimands, but their intervals are wide. Entry destination is an allocation conditional on moving from nonemployment to employment, not an employment-finding probability. The employed-to-employed switch frame says still less about hiring. For that reason, the mobility analysis, family-balanced disagreement statistics, rematching benchmark, and flow regressions appear in the online appendix rather than as a main contribution. No immediate-reversal rate is treated as a validated occupation-coding-error rate.
+Employment exit starts from employed origins; reported occupation change requires employment at both endpoints; entry destination conditions on nonemployment-to-employment links and measures allocation across exposure groups.  The last is neither a finding probability nor employer hiring.  Twelve-month links are endpoints, not sums of monthly transitions.  Among fixed-support employed origins, postperiod twelve-month retention ranges from 48.7 to 52.8 percent across young quintiles and from 63.9 to 70.2 percent across older quintiles; preperiod ranges are 50.1--54.0 and 68.8--73.3 percent.  Hours ($-0.119$, $[-0.295,0.057]$) and conditional weekly earnings ($-0.0245$, $[-0.1024,0.0534]$) are also imprecise and condition on employment; the legacy earnings series ends in March 2023 and contains only three postperiod months, while a full-window
+\texttt{EARNWEEK2} analysis remains unavailable in the authorized extracts.
+
+Observable poststratification does little to the adjacent employment-exit
+probability contrast: the descriptive Q5--Q1, young--older, post--pre difference
+is 0.0088 under official link weights and 0.0086 after reweighting retained
+links to the eligible-origin distribution.  At twelve months it moves from
+0.0166 to 0.0210.  This reweighting requires outcome missingness to be
+independent of linkage within the observed strata and includes a maximum
+untrimmed factor of 102.7.  Without that assumption, the jointly feasible
+linear accounting interval for twelve-month employment exit is
+$[-1.570,1.575]$, leaving the sign of selection bias unrestricted.  These are
+descriptive probability contrasts and missing-outcome bounds, not the nonlinear
+regression coefficient and its confidence interval.
+
+The annual primary rule excludes origins whose twelve-month link straddles the
+January 2023 onset.  Coding instead the fraction of destination months exposed
+gives an employment-exit coefficient of 0.158 ($[-0.070,0.387]$), compared
+with the Table~\ref{tab:flows} primary estimate of 0.123
+($[-0.111,0.357]$).  Both intervals contain zero;
+no paired interval for their difference is available.
+
+All flow exposure is defined at the origin where an origin occupation exists.  No current occupation is imputed to a nonemployed respondent.  Annual endpoints miss intervening jobs and transitions, and positive longitudinal-weight eligibility changes the population; neither horizon is a mechanical decomposition of the stock coefficient.  Link-rate and observable-balance differences diagnose selection but do not determine its direction, and alternative clustering addresses dependence rather than missing outcomes.
+
+\subsection{Payroll bridge and exposure architecture}
+
+BCC's August 2026 19-percent ``kept pace'' shortfall is a stock-growth comparison, not this regression coefficient or a hiring rate.  Complete public membership and tie rules are unavailable, so I use an equal-occupation, tie-preserving approximation and label it a bridge.  Its top-two-versus-bottom-three CPS coefficient is $-0.0720$ ($[-0.1223,-0.0218]$); family-month conditioning gives $-0.0168$ ($[-0.0808,0.0473]$).  Their paired movement is $0.0553$ ($[-0.0013,0.1118]$), so the design does not detect a difference.  Differences in universe, ages, outcome, endpoints, employer controls, and worker--firm flows remain.
+
+That grouping bridge is distinct from changing the regression objective.  Giving each of the 468 occupations equal total weight, while retaining \texttt{WTFINL} for within-cell age shares, changes the pooled Q5-by-post coefficient from $-0.1321$ to $-0.0833$; the paired difference is $0.0488$ ($[-0.0604,0.1581]$).  The family-month coefficient changes from $-0.0217$ to $-0.0047$, with paired difference $0.0170$ ($[-0.1587,0.1927]$).  These intervals are nondetections, not evidence that the objectives are equivalent.
+
+The bridge therefore asks whether a closer public grouping changes the CPS stock pattern; it does not calibrate CPS transitions to proprietary employer flows.  The required entry, exit, job-to-job, occupation-switching, aging, and denominator quantities are not jointly observed.
+
+Finally, $E_1+\lambda E_2$ varies the weight on tasks requiring complementary software.  Here $E_1$ is the directly accelerable task share and $E_2$ is the share requiring complementary software.  Each row uses rebuilt weights and common draws, and $\lambda=.5$ exactly reproduces beta.  Native-tail coefficients range from $-0.103$ at $\lambda=0$ to $-0.156$ at $\lambda=1$; every comparison with beta includes zero, even though 61.6 percent of stock changes quintile at $\lambda=0$.  This is large classification movement with limited coefficient resolution, not evidence about deployed technology.
+
+Five non-$\lambda$ definitions give pooled native-support tail coefficients from $-0.099$ (direct-ability AIOE) to $-0.014$ (the reversed OECD AI-skills gap).  Every alternative-minus-beta paired occupation interval contains zero, although the OECD lower endpoint is $-0.0004$.  On the common AIOE support, some family-conditioned implementation contrasts do exclude zero; the result is therefore sensitive to how one construct is mapped in that specification, without implying that the alternatives measure the same technology.
+

-\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:competing}
+\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}

-Public CPS data reproduce a negative young-relative employment-stock association under a widely used occupational AI-exposure construction. After repairing the calendar, the top-versus-bottom beta-quintile contrast is $-0.1346$ log points. Implementing the public top-two-versus-bottom-three grouping used by \citet{brynjolfsson2026canaries} gives a smaller but negative $-0.0728$ contrast. These are descriptive population comparisons, not replications of proprietary hiring results or causal AI estimates.
+Public CPS data contain a sizeable negative association between GPT-4 task exposure and employment for ages 22--25 relative to ages 26--65.  The rebuilt Q5--Q1 coefficient is $-0.132$ log points, but it falls to an imprecise $-0.022$ when broad occupation families receive their own young-relative monthly paths.  Their paired movement is detected; it is a change between conditional estimands, not a causal composition share.

-The decisive fact is where the comparison lies. Broad two-digit occupation-family controls reduce the detailed Q5--Q1 coefficient to about $-0.032$. The paired movement is detectable, while the residual coefficient is imprecise and draws on only 30 percent of baseline target information. Only four broad families span both exposure tails. Thus, the baseline pattern is principally a comparison across broad occupational families; the design does not precisely isolate a detailed within-family AI gradient.
+Direct within-family tails cover only 29 occupations and are highly imprecise.  Annual ACS data reduce person-sampling uncertainty but do not repair that support: the fixed-label full-calendar family-year coefficient is negative under ACS replicate inference, whereas occupation- and family-shock intervals include zero and the nonreuse-calendar family-year estimate is undetected.  Preperiod computer use strengthens rather than attenuates the coefficient, showing that computerization is not settled by one control.  Unrestricted dynamics reject joint preperiod equality, and linked-CPS flow estimates do not identify a mechanism or replicate employer-match hiring and separation.

-That conclusion should not be embellished into a mechanism the data reject. Low-exposure food-service occupations matter, but removing them attenuates the point estimate by less than one tenth. Broader in-person-service exclusions do not attenuate it. Joint influence exercises show large, oppositely signed occupation contributions rather than one occupation whose removal makes the result vanish. The evidence supports a broad-composition interpretation, not a demonstrated food-service-recovery explanation.
+Later self-reported use is descriptively aligned with the score.  An exact-code merge to a public RPS workbook retains 121 nonsuppressed occupations: the unweighted exposure--adoption correlation is 0.504 and mean adoption is 0.267 higher in Q5 than Q1.  The profile is not monotone, and only 26.2 percent of exposure variation remains after removing broad-family means.  Because the rates pool four 2025--26 waves and lack detailed-cell sampling uncertainty, this check validates neither adoption timing nor an employment mechanism.

-Nor do alternative exposure estimates permit a horse-race winner. All seven paired beta-minus-alternative Q5--Q1 intervals include zero, with minimum detectable differences as large as 0.169 log points. A confidence interval containing zero means that the design does not detect a difference; it does not establish economic equivalence. The original six implementations are also highly dependent, with two components accounting for 96.11 percent of their score variance. Claims of six independent validations or universal architecture robustness are therefore retired.
-
-The measurement work remains useful precisely because it distinguishes objects that should not be pooled. Exact-code matching across incompatible occupational vintages is an implementation error. Repairing scores on fixed support is a measurement comparison. Readmitting occupations changes the population. Switching from Q5--Q1 to top-two-versus-bottom-three changes the estimand. Moving from LLM task acceleration to patent overlap changes the construct. Rotating $F/G$ into $A/E$ changes only coordinates. The revised tables report these layers rather than treating every row as interchangeable robustness evidence.
-
-The paper's claim is consequently narrower and stronger than before. The early-career CPS pattern is real as a descriptive broad-occupation contrast, but this design cannot attribute it specifically to detailed AI exposure. Future causal work needs observed adoption or deployment, a credible source of timing or assignment variation, and outcomes that directly measure hiring and separation. Future measurement work needs occupation-task validation labels collected independently of the constructed indices, repeated ratings or audited use logs, and a taxonomy-stable holdout sample. Until then, occupational AI coefficients should travel with their construct, mapping, support, comparison, and resolution limits.
+The defensible conclusion is therefore comparison-specific.  Mapping, support, representation, and conditioning determine what an exposure coefficient measures.  A detected movement does not identify why it occurs; nondetection does not establish equivalence.  Causal work requires observed adoption or credible assignment and timing variation.  This paper instead establishes that the public-CPS young-worker pattern is principally a broad occupational comparison and quantifies what direct within-family and flow evidence cannot resolve.  Online Appendix I.3 collects the remaining data, design, and interpretation limits rather than treating them as additional specification searches.

-\textbf{Scope of this revision.} This document responds to every numbered major comment in the two reports supplied on September 5, 2026. +\section*{Response to the editor}

-\section*{Revision in one paragraph}
+We thank both referees for identifying the same central problem from different directions: the earlier manuscript reported a sizeable sensitivity to broad-family conditioning, but it did not yet make occupational support and the precise descriptive target the organizing contribution.  We have substantially refocused the paper.  Its positive result is now that an apparently broad national exposure comparison contains little direct within-family tail information.  On the primary 468-occupation support, only four broad families contain both Q1 and Q5 occupations; the resulting 29 direct-tail occupations represent 5.03 percent of preperiod employment stock, and their information is equivalent to 6.2 occupations.  The paper treats the pooled-to-conditioned coefficient movement as a consequence of that support geometry, not as a causal ``share explained'' or evidence against an AI effect.

-We accepted the central diagnosis and rewrote the paper around the result the CPS can establish. The corrected Q5--Q1 beta coefficient is $-.1346$, but it falls to $-.0315$ after broad-family-specific post changes are absorbed and to $-.0317$ after family-specific monthly paths are absorbed. The paired movement is detectable, although the conditional estimand changes and retains only 30 percent of baseline target information. Clean service exclusions do not support a food-service mechanism. We now report a primary MDE$_{80}$ of .1244 and paired MDEs for every architecture comparison; all seven paired Q5--Q1 intervals include zero. The title, abstract, introduction, results, and conclusion no longer claim general architecture heterogeneity or AI-specific displacement. The revised contribution is a composition-centered empirical finding joined to a framework that separates implementation error, measurement, construct, support, estimand, representation, and reparameterization changes.
+The revision adds the analyses required to make that claim auditable.  It places the family-by-quintile support matrix and information diagnostics in the main paper; reports 89 supported family-specific edges and a broader 490-occupation support check; gives exact young/older and within-family/family-weight stock accounting; crosses computer-use and family conditioning on common support; separates the static coefficient from reference-dependent and reference-invariant dynamic functionals; completes a target-specific finite-sample audit; adds age-band, fixed-age-composition, enrollment, cohort-composition, flow-selection, ACS, public-benchmark, and later-adoption checks; and resolves the earlier numerical-existence question for all eleven declared models.  The manuscript also distinguishes results that are informative under one sampling target from those that are robust across occupation, family, household, and ACS-replicate perturbations.

-\section*{Response to Referee 1}
+Several requests required modification rather than nominal compliance.  We do not promote December 2024 to the primary endpoint after observing the outcomes; it is instead a prominent pre-2025-production-break benchmark beside the unchanged full-window target.  We do not manufacture an Oster-style scalar bound for a nonlinear grouped-binomial coefficient when the required selection relation is not identified.  Exact BCC occupation memberships and tie handling are not public, so our BCC exercises remain labeled public-data analogues rather than replications.  Full-window weekly earnings remain unavailable because the authorized extracts omit \texttt{EARNWEEK2}; the short legacy series is retained only as ancillary evidence.  These non-adoptions and input constraints are stated below rather than described as completed work.

-\subsection*{M1. Precision and comparative language}
+\section*{Response to Referee A: major comments}

-\emph{Response.} Accepted. The main text now reports the realized primary MDE$_{80}$ of .1244 log points next to the $-.1311$ frozen estimate. A consolidated paired table reports MDEs from .0609 to .1689 for beta against the three AIOE implementations, alpha, broad, Webb AI, and OECD. Every paired Q5--Q1 interval contains zero. Throughout, we now write ``the design does not detect a difference'' rather than ``the conclusion does not generalize,'' and never infer equivalence.
+\subsection*{3.1. Family conditioning, support, and the order of the argument}

-We also diagnosed the prospective-to-realized SE gap. A sharp historical simulation preserves each donor month's full contemporaneous cross-occupation residual vector with a global sign. Its mean SE remains .01217, so it closes none of the gap to .04441. We report this as a rejection of one proposed mechanism, not a decomposition or validation of the planning simulation. See Sections 1, 4, and 6; Main Table 3 and Figure 4; Appendix D and K.
+\emph{Response: accepted.}  The abstract, introduction, and first results section now lead with support and information rather than the near-zero conditioned coefficient.  The main paper displays the family-by-quintile matrix and reports that only four of 22 families span Q1 and Q5, that the 29 direct-tail occupations account for 5.03 percent of preperiod stock, that family-month conditioning retains 29.7 percent of pooled Q5 target information, and that the direct-tail effective count is 6.2 occupations with a 77.8-percent top-five information share.  The direct-tail interval, $[-0.169,0.468]$, is presented before interpreting the conditioned coefficient.

-\subsection*{M2. Effective clustering and CPS rotation}
+The introduction now states explicitly that family paths cannot distinguish an AI effect operating at the broad-family level from unrelated family-level evolution.  The movement from $-0.1321$ to $-0.0217$ is therefore a comparison of conditional estimands and a corollary of the support geometry.  It is not a causal decomposition, and the residual coefficient is not evidence that AI has no effect.

-\emph{Response.} Addressed within the available survey design. Young-cell respondent equivalents have p10/median/p90 of 0/2/16; 26.3 percent are zero and 69.9 percent below five. Quarterly aggregation gives $-.13103$ on the frozen and $-.13454$ on the repaired calendar, nearly identical to monthly estimates and SEs. A cross-occupation time-score covariance sensitivity increases the SE to .04909 at a 12-month Newey--West lag, with normal interval $[-.2273,-.0349]$.
+\subsection*{3.2. Pandemic quarters and preperiod diagnostics}

-The main text now explains that occupation clustering already permits arbitrary serial dependence within occupation but is not a complete CPS design-based variance. The additional HAC targets common monthly and nearby-month score covariance across occupations. The extract begins in 2017, so literal 2015--19 pseudo-breaks are unavailable. We run all feasible 2017--19 dates and emphasize 12 balanced dates; none approaches $-.1311$. Their $1/13$ empirical tail is explicitly a resolution floor in a dependent, post-outcome diagnostic, not a randomization $p$-value. See Section 4 and Appendix D.
+\emph{Response: accepted, with the nulls kept distinct.}  The appendix now reports fixed tests for 2017Q1--2019Q4, 2021Q1--2022Q3, and the full preperiod excluding 2020Q2--2020Q4, under both the short-reference null and a reference-invariant within-window equality null.  Over the full preperiod, the reference-invariant test rejects for the pooled model ($p=.0121$) but not for the family-conditioned model ($p=.0641$).  After excluding 2020Q2--2020Q4, neither within-window equality test rejects at five percent ($p=.0676$ pooled and $p=.1048$ conditioned).  Relative to the short 2022Q4 reference, the corresponding pandemic-exclusion values are $p=.0772$ and $p=.0079$.  The difference is reported as a property of the null and the unusually short reference period, not as a favorable re-selection of the window.

-\subsection*{M3. Regression analogue of the permutation, monotonicity, and stable tails}
+Leave-one-quarter diagnostics identify 2019Q2 as producing the largest reduction in the pooled reference-relative Wald statistic and 2018Q4 in the conditioned statistic.  Thus the revised paper does not say that rejection is confined to pandemic quarters.  It says that the full pooled within-window rejection no longer clears five percent when those quarters are excluded, while the reference-relative family rejection persists.  HonestDiD is now run on the full event vector and covariance for the explicitly named post functional $P$, with both the full calibration and the consecutive 2021Q1--2022Q3 calibration.  Its conventional intervals already include zero, and the first positive smoothness allowance also includes zero; the exercise is not presented as a robustness statement for the static coefficient.

-\emph{Response.} This result now determines the paper. On the corrected calendar, SOC2-by-young-by-post terms reduce $-.1346$ to $-.0315$; SOC2-by-young-by-month paths give $-.0317$. Paired baseline-to-conditioned movements are approximately .103 and their intervals exclude zero. We do not call this a causal decomposition: conditioning changes the estimand, only four of 22 families span Q1 and Q5, and target information falls to 30 percent.
+\subsection*{3.3. Young numerator and older denominator}

-We add a three-restriction test of a common Q2--Q5 coefficient ($p=.119$) and a least-favorable max-$t$ monotonicity assessment ($p=.393$). Neither equality nor monotonicity is established. The 46 always-Q1 and 18 always-Q5 occupations yield $-.2120$ with interval $[-.4131,-.0109]$, but only 15.0 effective information occupations and a 46.7-percent top-five share. See Section 5, Main Table 2 and Figure 2, and Appendix C/E.
+\emph{Response: addressed through exact stock accounting rather than unmatched regressions.}  Under the saturated occupation--month nuisance structure, separate single-age regressions do not reproduce the ratio target: the corresponding single-age target is absorbed, while an additive occupation-plus-month regression would define a different model.  We therefore report an exact, model-free equal-month accounting on the same Q1 and Q5 stocks.  The Q5--Q1 young-stock change is $-0.1098$ log point and the older-stock change is $0.0483$, so their young-relative difference is $-0.1581$.  The numerator supplies the larger movement and the denominator reinforces it.  A second exact Shapley accounting assigns $-0.1293$ to within-family ratio changes and $-0.0288$ to changing older-family weights, with a zero boundary term after auditing all absent cells.

-\subsection*{M4. Influence concentration and service recovery}
+These identities are reported in the main paper and appendix, but are not forced to equal the nonlinear grouped-binomial coefficient $-0.1321$.  We label them descriptive stock accounting, not separate causal regressions or percentage shares.

-\emph{Response.} Influence is now a headline diagnostic rather than summarized by the absence of sign reversal. Deleting the five occupations with largest absolute prior LOCO movements gives $-.1011$. Deleting ten or twenty gives $-.1522$ and $-.1555$, showing offsetting signed influence. A symmetric signed-tail trim gives $-.1281$ and continuous Huber down-weighting $-.1314$. These data-adaptive rows are labeled diagnostics, not preferred estimators.
+\subsection*{3.4. Finite-sample stress results}

-We replaced the coarse industry proxy with occupation-major-group exclusions. Dropping Q1 food-service occupations gives $-.1196$, an 8.8-percent attenuation. Dropping broader in-person services gives $-.1370$ or $-.1387$. We therefore reject the proposed strong food-service mechanism while accepting that the Q1 trajectory and individual occupations matter. See Section 5 and Appendix E/J.
+\emph{Response: accepted.}  We withdrew the earlier 26.7- and 11.3-percent figures rather than defending them.  The replacement audit has eleven declared designs, 799--1,599 retained outer replications per design, 9,999 common multiplier draws per joint refit, independently computed pseudo-targets, and separate occupation, family, and cross-fit-oracle procedures.  In the empirical observed-effect design, occupation-wild coverage is 0.865, 0.921, and 0.889 for the pooled, family-conditioned, and paired targets; family-Rademacher coverage is 0.990, 0.895, and 0.992.  In the clean zero-target design, rejection of the family-conditioned target is 6.1 percent under occupation weights, 8.9 percent under family weights, and 5.6 percent for the cross-fit oracle.

-\subsection*{M5. CPS population controls}
+The simulation also isolates why a structural-null calibration can remain far from a zero pooled projection: removing the structural exposure coefficient while retaining the fitted family-month surface leaves a pooled pseudo-target of $-0.11035$.  The revision therefore does not offer one omnibus ``size-corrected'' interval.  It reports target-specific performance and states the stronger limitation: no feasible cluster procedure is validated for every target and calibrated design, and rejection of the pooled projection alone is not a test of a structural exposure effect.

-\emph{Response.} The respondent-equivalent result is promoted and defined operationally as cells reconstructed from observed records without final CPS weights. It is not a common rescaling of weighted stocks. On the repaired calendar it is $-.1348$, versus $-.1346$ under official weights. Ending in December 2024 gives $-.1056$ and $-.1135$, respectively.
+\subsection*{3.5. Computer use, collinearity, and sensitivity}

-BLS does not publish age-by-occupation bridge weights for the January 2025 revision; its experimental factors are aggregate. We therefore do not fabricate a counterfactual micro-weight series. The raw official-weight contrast becomes slightly less negative between December 2024 and January 2025, and the official 2025--26 era is not distinguishable from 2023--24. The documentation, bounded inference, and refusal are now explicit. See Section 7 and Appendix F.
+\emph{Response: accepted in substance; the proposed scalar bound was not imposed.}  On a fixed 455-occupation support, the paper now reports the four matched models: baseline, computer use, family-month paths, and both together, all with common draws.  Computer use moves the exposure coefficient from $-0.0958$ to $-0.1966$; family paths alone give $0.0354$; both give $-0.0467$.  The computer-use coefficient itself is $0.0460$ per preperiod-weighted standard deviation in the pooled computer model and $0.0487$ in the combined model, with both intervals reported.  The beta--computer-use correlation is 0.797 on this support, retained Q5 information falls from 69.1 to 29.0 percent, and the VIF-like ratio is 3.45.

-\subsection*{M6. Selected architecture space}
+The text treats the positive computer coefficient and the more negative exposure coefficient as joint-projection facts under collinearity, not as evidence that computerization raises employment or that the residual isolates AI.  We added an explicit applicability ruling for Oster, Cinelli--Hazlett, and Diegert--Masten--Poirier style analyses: none maps automatically to this nonlinear grouped-binomial target with generated, highly collinear treatments and changing nuisance spaces.  Rather than choose an arbitrary proportional-selection parameter after outcomes were known, we report the matched projection matrix and information loss and leave a scalar confounder bound unreported.

It does not claim a global count.
